activities quietly.\n  manifestation: At book club or during quiet evenings with my husband, I have a hard time just sitting quietly with the book or the movie, and before I know it I'm commenting, rustling in the kitchen, or suggesting we do something else.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: Since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I move from lesson planning to laundry to softball practice to chopping food for a community event without much pause, even on nights I promised myself I would rest.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I notice I can take over a conversation in the Spanish department or at the It’s not severe, but it’s happening enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up, or does it feel like you're getting up without really deciding to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I’ve gotten up before I’ve really decided to. It’s more that I get physically restless. Like when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays or in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Then I have to remind myself to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or irritable when you're unable to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I get upset, no. It’s more just feeling restless than angry.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities, such as reading or listening to music?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. At book club, or even just quiet evenings at home with my husband, I have a hard time just sitting with the book or the movie. Before I know it I’m making comments, or getting up to rustle around in the kitchen, or suggesting we do something else. It’s not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself when you get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’ll notice I’ve gotten up when everyone else is still seated, like in a meeting or at dinner, and then I feel a bit embarrassed or criticize myself for it. It’s more that I get physically restless. Like when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays or in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Then I have to remind myself to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or irritable when you're unable to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I get upset, no. Maybe a little impatient if I have to sit still for a long time, but I wouldn’t say irritable exactly. It’s more just feeling restless than angry.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities, such as reading or listening to music?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. At book club, or even just quiet evenings at home with my husband, I have a hard time just sitting with the book or the movie. Before I know it I’m making comments, or getting up to rustle around in the kitchen, or suggesting we do something else. It’s not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself when you get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’ll notice I’ve gotten up when everyone else is still seated, like in a meeting or at dinner, and then I feel a bit embarrassed or criticize myself for it. I’ll tell myself, “Why couldn’t you just stay put?” Sometimes I make an excuse, like needing to refill coffee or check a handout, when really nothing was urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up and moving around because you want to be active, or is it more that you just can't seem to sit still?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I just can’t seem to sit still. I don’t think, “Oh, I want to be active now.” It’s more like I’m in a meeting or at dinner and I suddenly find myself needing to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even though nothing really needs doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood or when you're feeling neutral?\n\nPatient: It seems to happen even when my mood is fine, honestly. I can be feeling perfectly neutral, or even in a good mood, and still I’ll get up during a meeting or dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll tell myself I need to refill my coffee or check a handout, but really there isn’t anything urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel stressed or overwhelmed when you're feeling restless and get up to move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. It’s not always that I’m overwhelmed first, but when I notice I’ve gotten up again in a meeting or at dinner, it can make me feel stressed or embarrassed. I’ll be thinking, “Why am I doing this again?” even if it’s just something like refilling coffee or checking a handout when it didn’t really need to be done.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "determine if the patient's restlessness is associated with any emotional distress", "question": "Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of pressure or stress?"}

In meetings or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even when there isn’t really any urgent reason to. It’s not severe, but it’s happening enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up, or does it feel like you're getting up without really deciding to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I’ve gotten up before I’ve really decided to. I’ll be sitting in a meeting or at dinner, and suddenly I’m on my feet to refill coffee or look at a handout, and then I realize there wasn’t actually any urgent reason to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you're sitting still in those situations?\n\nPatient: Not anxious, exactly. It’s more that I get physically restless. Like when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays or in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Then I have to remind myself to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or irritable when you're unable to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I get upset, no. Then I have to remind myself to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or irritable when you're unable to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I get upset, no. Maybe a little impatient if I have to sit still for a long time, but I wouldn’t say irritable exactly. It’s more just feeling restless than angry.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities, such as reading or listening to music?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. At book club, or even just quiet evenings at home with my husband, I have a hard time just sitting with the book or the movie. Before I know it I’m making comments, or getting up to rustle around in the kitchen, or suggesting we do something else. It’s not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself when you get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’ll notice I’ve gotten up when everyone else is still seated, like in a meeting or at dinner, and then I feel a bit embarrassed or criticize myself for it. I’ll tell myself, “Why couldn’t you just stay put?” Sometimes I make an excuse, like needing to refill coffee or check a handout, when really nothing was urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up and moving around because you want to be active, or is it more that you just can't seem to sit still?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I just can’t seem to sit still. I don’t think, “Oh, I want to be active now.” It’s more like I’m in a meeting or at dinner and I suddenly find myself needing to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even though nothing really needs doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood or when you're feeling neutral?\n\nPatient: It seems to happen even when my mood is fine, honestly. I can be feeling perfectly neutral, or even in a good mood, and still I’ll get up during a meeting or dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll tell myself I need to refill my coffee or check a handout, but really there isn’t anything urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel stressed or overwhelmed when you're feeling restless and get up to move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. It’s not always that I’m overwhelmed first, but when I notice I’ve gotten up again in a meeting or at dinner, it can make me feel stressed or embarrassed. I’ll be thinking, “Why am I doing this again?” even if it’s just something like refilling coffee or checking a handout when it didn’t really need to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of pressure or stress?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, but it’s not only when I’m under pressure. The main thing that brought me in is that I keep getting up in meetings or at dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll suddenly think I need to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even when nothing is really urgent.\n\nDoctor: How often do you experience these episodes of restlessness in a typical week?\n\nPatient: In a typical week, I’d say it happens a few times. Mostly in situations where I’m supposed to stay seated, like meetings or dinner. I’ll get up to refill coffee, check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room, and then realize it wasn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I was a girl, my report cards would say I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’ll tell myself, “Why couldn’t you just stay put?” Sometimes I make an excuse, like needing to refill coffee or check a handout, when really nothing was urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up and moving around because you want to be active, or is it more that you just can't seem to sit still?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I just can’t seem to sit still. I don’t think, “Oh, I want to be active now.” It’s more like I’m in a meeting or at dinner and I suddenly find myself needing to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even though nothing really needs doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood or when you're feeling neutral?\n\nPatient: It seems to happen even when my mood is fine, honestly. I can be feeling perfectly neutral, or even in a good mood, and still I’ll get up during a meeting or dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll tell myself I need to refill my coffee or check a handout, but really there isn’t anything urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel stressed or overwhelmed when you're feeling restless and get up to move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. It’s not always that I’m overwhelmed first, but when I notice I’ve gotten up again in a meeting or at dinner, it can make me feel stressed or embarrassed. I’ll be thinking, “Why am I doing this again?” even if it’s just something like refilling coffee or checking a handout when it didn’t really need to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of pressure or stress?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, but it’s not only when I’m under pressure. The main thing that brought me in is that I keep getting up in meetings or at dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll suddenly think I need to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even when nothing is really urgent.\n\nDoctor: How often do you experience these episodes of restlessness in a typical week?\n\nPatient: In a typical week, I’d say it happens a few times. Mostly in situations where I’m supposed to stay seated, like meetings or dinner. I’ll get up to refill coffee, check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room, and then realize it wasn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I was a girl, my report cards would say I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention or following through on instructions when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I haven’t really noticed trouble following instructions just because I’m sitting still. It’s more that sitting still itself can feel hard, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

path between my desk and the pencil sharpener before I was ten.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: It has started to affect my relationships at work and in my community because people lose patience with me in meetings and book club, and I leave feeling embarrassed instead of connected.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably harder to manage for about eight months, since my youngest left for college and the house became quieter.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S011\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I was a girl, my report cards would say I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention or following through on instructions when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I haven’t really noticed trouble following instructions just because I’m sitting still. It’s more that sitting still itself can feel hard, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on what's being discussed when you're sitting still in meetings or other quiet activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it trouble focusing. I can usually follow what’s being discussed. It’s more that I’m aware of wanting to move or shift around, but I don’t think I lose the thread of the meeting very often.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas this feeling of restlessness and inability to stay seated been a consistent part of your behavior since childhood?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve come in because I’ve noticed I keep getting up when I’m supposed to stay seated. In meetings or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even when there isn’t really any urgent reason to. It’s not severe, but it’s happening enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up, or does it feel like you're getting up without really deciding to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I’ve gotten up before I’ve really decided to. I’ll be sitting in a meeting or at dinner, and suddenly I’m on my feet to refill coffee or look at a handout, and then I realize there wasn’t actually any urgent reason to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you're sitting still in those situations?\n\nPatient: Not anxious, exactly. It’s more that I get physically restless. Like when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays or in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Then I have to remind myself to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or irritable when you're unable to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I get upset, no. Maybe a little impatient if I have to sit still for a long time, but I wouldn’t say irritable exactly. It’s more just feeling restless than angry.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities, such as reading or listening to music?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. At book club, or even just quiet evenings at home with my husband, I have a hard time just sitting with the book or the movie. Before I know it I’m making comments, or getting up to rustle around in the kitchen, or suggesting we do something else. It’s not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself when you get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’ll notice I’ve gotten up when everyone else is still seated, like in a meeting or at dinner, and then I feel a bit embarrassed or criticize myself for it. I’ll tell myself, “Why couldn’t you just stay put?” Sometimes I make an excuse, like needing to refill coffee or check a handout, when really nothing was urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up and moving around because you want to be active, or is it more that you just can't seem to sit still?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I just can’t seem to sit still. I don’t think, “Oh, I want to be active now.” It’s more like I’m in a meeting or at dinner and I suddenly find myself needing to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even though nothing really needs doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood or when you're feeling neutral?\n\nPatient: It seems to happen even when my mood is fine, honestly. I can be feeling perfectly neutral, or even in a good mood, and still I’ll get up during a meeting or dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll tell myself I need to refill my coffee or check a handout, but really there isn’t anything urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel stressed or overwhelmed when you're feeling restless and get up to move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. It’s not always that I’m overwhelmed first, but when I notice I’ve gotten up again in a meeting or at dinner, it can make me feel stressed or embarrassed. I’ll be thinking, “Why am I doing this again?” even if it’s just something like refilling coffee or checking a handout when it didn’t really need to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of pressure or stress?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, but it’s not only when I’m under pressure. The main thing that brought me in is that I keep getting up in meetings or at dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll suddenly think I need to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even when nothing is really urgent.\n\nDoctor: How often do you experience these episodes of restlessness in a typical week?\n\nPatient: In a typical week, I’d say it happens a few times. Mostly in situations where I’m supposed to stay seated, like meetings or dinner. I’ll get up to refill coffee, check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room, and then realize it wasn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I was a girl, my report cards would say I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention or following through on instructions when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I haven’t really noticed trouble following instructions just because I’m sitting still. It’s more that sitting still itself can feel hard, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on what's being discussed when you're sitting still in meetings or other quiet activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it trouble focusing. I can usually follow what’s being discussed. It’s more that I’m aware of wanting to move or shift around, but I don’t think I lose the thread of the meeting very often.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of restlessness and inability to stay seated been a consistent part of your behavior since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I know I mentioned it, but it really does go back that far. As a girl my report cards often said I was bright, but couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve come in because I’ve noticed I keep getting up when I’m supposed to stay seated. In meetings or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even when there isn’t really any urgent reason to. It’s not severe, but it’s happening enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up, or does it feel like you're getting up without really deciding to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I’ve gotten up before I’ve really decided to. I’ll be sitting in a meeting or at dinner, and suddenly I’m on my feet to refill coffee or look at a handout, and then I realize there wasn’t actually any urgent reason to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you're sitting still in those situations?\n\nPatient: Not anxious, exactly. It’s more that I get physically restless. Like when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays or in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Then I have to remind myself to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or irritable when you're unable to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I get upset, no. Maybe a little impatient if I have to sit still for a long time, but I wouldn’t say irritable exactly. It’s more just feeling restless than angry.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities, such as reading or listening to music?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. At book club, or even just quiet evenings at home with my husband, I have a hard time just sitting with the book or the movie. Before I know it I’m making comments, or getting up to rustle around in the kitchen, or suggesting we do something else. It’s not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself when you get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’ll notice I’ve gotten up when everyone else is still seated, like in a meeting or at dinner, and then I feel a bit embarrassed or criticize myself for it. I’ll tell myself, “Why couldn’t you just stay put?” Sometimes I make an excuse, like needing to refill coffee or check a handout, when really nothing was urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up and moving around because you want to be active, or is it more that you just can't seem to sit still?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I just can’t seem to sit still. I don’t think, “Oh, I want to be active now.” It’s more like I’m in a meeting or at dinner and I suddenly find myself needing to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even though nothing really needs doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood or when you're feeling neutral?\n\nPatient: It seems to happen even when my mood is fine, honestly. I can be feeling perfectly neutral, or even in a good mood, and still I’ll get up during a meeting or dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll tell myself I need to refill my coffee or check a handout, but really there isn’t anything urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel stressed or overwhelmed when you're feeling restless and get up to move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. It’s not always that I’m overwhelmed first, but when I notice I’ve gotten up again in a meeting or at dinner, it can make me feel stressed or embarrassed. I’ll be thinking, “Why am I doing this again?” even if it’s just something like refilling coffee or checking a handout when it didn’t really need to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of pressure or stress?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, but it’s not only when I’m under pressure. The main thing that brought me in is that I keep getting up in meetings or at dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll suddenly think I need to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even when nothing is really urgent.\n\nDoctor: How often do you experience these episodes of restlessness in a typical week?\n\nPatient: In a typical week, I’d say it happens a few times. Mostly in situations where I’m supposed to stay seated, like meetings or dinner. I’ll get up to refill coffee, check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room, and then realize it wasn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I was a girl, my report cards would say I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention or following through on instructions when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I haven’t really noticed trouble following instructions just because I’m sitting still. It’s more that sitting still itself can feel hard, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on what's being discussed when you're sitting still in meetings or other quiet activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it trouble focusing. I can usually follow what’s being discussed. It’s more that I’m aware of wanting to move or shift around, but I don’t think I lose the thread of the meeting very often.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of restlessness and inability to stay seated been a consistent part of your behavior since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I know I mentioned it, but it really does go back that far. As a girl my report cards often said I was bright, but couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're supposed to be focused on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s especially common for me. I mean, I notice things around me like anyone would, but I haven’t really felt that being distracted by noises or movement is a major issue when I’m meant to be focused.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Before I know it I’m making comments, or getting up to rustle around in the kitchen, or suggesting we do something else. It’s not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself when you get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’ll notice I’ve gotten up when everyone else is still seated, like in a meeting or at dinner, and then I feel a bit embarrassed or criticize myself for it. I’ll tell myself, “Why couldn’t you just stay put?” Sometimes I make an excuse, like needing to refill coffee or check a handout, when really nothing was urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up and moving around because you want to be active, or is it more that you just can't seem to sit still?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I just can’t seem to sit still. I don’t think, “Oh, I want to be active now.” It’s more like I’m in a meeting or at dinner and I suddenly find myself needing to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even though nothing really needs doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood or when you're feeling neutral?\n\nPatient: It seems to happen even when my mood is fine, honestly. I can be feeling perfectly neutral, or even in a good mood, and still I’ll get up during a meeting or dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll tell myself I need to refill my coffee or check a handout, but really there isn’t anything urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel stressed or overwhelmed when you're feeling restless and get up to move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. It’s not always that I’m overwhelmed first, but when I notice I’ve gotten up again in a meeting or at dinner, it can make me feel stressed or embarrassed. I’ll be thinking, “Why am I doing this again?” even if it’s just something like refilling coffee or checking a handout when it didn’t really need to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of pressure or stress?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, but it’s not only when I’m under pressure. The main thing that brought me in is that I keep getting up in meetings or at dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll suddenly think I need to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even when nothing is really urgent.\n\nDoctor: How often do you experience these episodes of restlessness in a typical week?\n\nPatient: In a typical week, I’d say it happens a few times. Mostly in situations where I’m supposed to stay seated, like meetings or dinner. I’ll get up to refill coffee, check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room, and then realize it wasn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I was a girl, my report cards would say I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention or following through on instructions when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I haven’t really noticed trouble following instructions just because I’m sitting still. It’s more that sitting still itself can feel hard, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on what's being discussed when you're sitting still in meetings or other quiet activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it trouble focusing. I can usually follow what’s being discussed. It’s more that I’m aware of wanting to move or shift around, but I don’t think I lose the thread of the meeting very often.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of restlessness and inability to stay seated been a consistent part of your behavior since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I know I mentioned it, but it really does go back that far. As a girl my report cards often said I was bright, but couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're supposed to be focused on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s especially common for me. I mean, I notice things around me like anyone would, but I haven’t really felt that being distracted by noises or movement is a major issue when I’m meant to be focused.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind starts to wander or you have trouble paying attention when you're sitting still and feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I can feel restless sitting still, but I don’t usually notice my mind wandering because of it. I might want to shift around or get up, but I can usually still pay attention.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.8, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve come in because I’ve noticed I keep getting up when I’m supposed to stay seated. In meetings or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even when there isn’t really any urgent reason to. It’s not severe, but it’s happening enough that I thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up, or does it feel like you're getting up without really deciding to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I’ve gotten up before I’ve really decided to. I’ll be sitting in a meeting or at dinner, and suddenly I’m on my feet to refill coffee or look at a handout, and then I realize there wasn’t actually any urgent reason to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you're sitting still in those situations?\n\nPatient: Not anxious, exactly. It’s more that I get physically restless. Like when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays or in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Then I have to remind myself to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or irritable when you're unable to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I get upset, no. Maybe a little impatient if I have to sit still for a long time, but I wouldn’t say irritable exactly. It’s more just feeling restless than angry.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities, such as reading or listening to music?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. At book club, or even just quiet evenings at home with my husband, I have a hard time just sitting with the book or the movie. Before I know it I’m making comments, or getting up to rustle around in the kitchen, or suggesting we do something else. It’s not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or criticize yourself when you get up and move around in situations where you're supposed to stay seated?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’ll notice I’ve gotten up when everyone else is still seated, like in a meeting or at dinner, and then I feel a bit embarrassed or criticize myself for it. I’ll tell myself, “Why couldn’t you just stay put?” Sometimes I make an excuse, like needing to refill coffee or check a handout, when really nothing was urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're getting up and moving around because you want to be active, or is it more that you just can't seem to sit still?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I just can’t seem to sit still. I don’t think, “Oh, I want to be active now.” It’s more like I’m in a meeting or at dinner and I suddenly find myself needing to refill my coffee, or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even though nothing really needs doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're in a good mood or when you're feeling neutral?\n\nPatient: It seems to happen even when my mood is fine, honestly. I can be feeling perfectly neutral, or even in a good mood, and still I’ll get up during a meeting or dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll tell myself I need to refill my coffee or check a handout, but really there isn’t anything urgent.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel stressed or overwhelmed when you're feeling restless and get up to move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. It’s not always that I’m overwhelmed first, but when I notice I’ve gotten up again in a meeting or at dinner, it can make me feel stressed or embarrassed. I’ll be thinking, “Why am I doing this again?” even if it’s just something like refilling coffee or checking a handout when it didn’t really need to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel restless when you're under a lot of pressure or stress?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, but it’s not only when I’m under pressure. The main thing that brought me in is that I keep getting up in meetings or at dinner when everyone else is seated. I’ll suddenly think I need to refill coffee, check a handout, or walk to the back of the room, even when nothing is really urgent.\n\nDoctor: How often do you experience these episodes of restlessness in a typical week?\n\nPatient: In a typical week, I’d say it happens a few times. Mostly in situations where I’m supposed to stay seated, like meetings or dinner. I’ll get up to refill coffee, check a handout, or just walk to the back of the room, and then realize it wasn’t really necessary.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this kind of restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I was a girl, my report cards would say I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention or following through on instructions when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I haven’t really noticed trouble following instructions just because I’m sitting still. It’s more that sitting still itself can feel hard, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on what's being discussed when you're sitting still in meetings or other quiet activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it trouble focusing. I can usually follow what’s being discussed. It’s more that I’m aware of wanting to move or shift around, but I don’t think I lose the thread of the meeting very often.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of restlessness and inability to stay seated been a consistent part of your behavior since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I know I mentioned it, but it really does go back that far. As a girl my report cards often said I was bright, but couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're supposed to be focused on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s especially common for me. I mean, I notice things around me like anyone would, but I haven’t really felt that being distracted by noises or movement is a major issue when I’m meant to be focused.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind starts to wander or you have trouble paying attention when you're sitting still and feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I can feel restless sitting still, but I don’t usually notice my mind wandering because of it. I might want to shift around or get up, but I can usually still pay attention.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of restlessness and getting up during meetings or dinner ever caused problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has caused some problems. It’s not constant, but people at work have lost patience with me in meetings when I’m up and down or seem unable to sit through things. And in my book club too, I can tell it irritates people sometimes.\n\nI usually leave feeling embarrassed, like I didn’t connect with people the way I wanted to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. The main way it shows up is in conversations. In group discussions, like at book club or curriculum meetings, I’ll jump in before someone has finished what they’re saying. The answer or comment is already in my head, and I blurt it out before I’ve really thought about how it might come across. I don’t mean to be rude, but I can see afterward that I’ve interrupted.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
